But sometimes a soloist or two advanced dancers with a good enough connection can improvise in a contemporary routine. That’s the kind of energy it has. I’m more concerned with loosening up a bit of your rigidity.”

    “Rigidity is sort of my bread and butter,” Lena mutters. Kara squeezes her shoulders encouragingly.

    “Come on, I beg to differ. You can’t really still think that after last week,” Kara says, seemingly totally unaware of how her easy admiration always affects Lena. “You’ve so grown past that.”

    Lena swallows hard. “I think I’m just nervous to do something that isn’t ballroom. Ballroom at least has norms I can follow.”

    “Contemporary does have norms, it just also tends as a genre to break those norms,” Kara explains. It doesn’t do much to soothe Lena’s anxiety. “It’s common for choreographers to throw it all out the window and try to create something new.”

    Lena nods. Everything about contemporary sounds terrifying, if she’s being honest, but Kara is so enthusiastic about it that she can’t bring herself to rain on her parade. And it’s impossible not to absorb a little bit of her confidence.

    “It’s the heart of what I really love to do,” Kara continues, and her hands move down to rest on Lena’s upper arms in a warm, loose grip. “A story in movement.”

    “What kind of story?” Lena asks. She’s pretty sure she knows the answer.

    “A love story,” Kara says.

    Of course.

    The routine Kara and Imra lay out is intimate. All their numbers have been so to an extent, but this one feels almost uncomfortable for Lena to watch – Kara and Imra perform it barefoot, moving their bodies to the chosen acoustic Coldplay cover in ways Lena can’t predict, and the music is nice but difficult to find a consistent beat in. The movements look almost discordant in Lena’s eyes. The routine is about unrequited love, the choreography following the storytelling in the song lyrics in a dramatic push and pull until it ends with Imra cradled intimately against Kara’s front being held in a tight, loving grip with Kara’s mouth near her ear.

    Through most of it Kara keeps her eyes on Lena rather than Imra as if, like Lena, she can’t express the routine properly with anyone else.

    Kara whispers something in Imra’s ear as the song fades out, a low and private-looking murmur that Lena can’t hear, and it might have triggered a hint of unfounded jealousy if Imra didn’t immediately burst into laughter.

    “What?” Lena asks, folding her arms to ease the tightness in her chest. “What’s so funny?”

    “I said her boyfriend is stinky,” Kara says matter-of-factly, hopping back to her feet. Imra snorts.

    “The whisper is part of the choreography,” Imra explains, letting Kara help her back up to standing with an overdramatic heave. “The cameras will pick up Kara’s lips moving but not what she says, and people can interpret it however they want to round out the number.”

    “Ideally they’ll interpret it as the end of the story. A love confession,” Kara says, her gaze suddenly turning away from Lena. Her cheeks are a little pink, and she busies herself with her phone.

    “So what are you actually going to say to me on stage?” Lena asks. Kara doesn’t seem to be doing anything on her phone besides scrolling up and down her playlist, and Kara shrugs before closing the app and reopening it.

    “No idea! I’ll think of something.”

    “Better not be something funny or it’ll ruin the whole vibe when Lena laughs at you,” Imra warns. Kara seems unbothered.

    Lena does struggle at first, when Imra starts showing her the breakdown of the individual movements. She struggles to loosen up, to be vulnerable and unstructured in the way she knows Kara wants. But Kara makes her laugh at the end of every run-through with her dramatic whispers – ‘I have to pee’ or ‘what do you want for dinner’, or even once pulling her close and quietly singing the Duck Tales theme song in her ear until Lena bursts into giggles. It takes the pressure off, leaves her relaxed at the top of each run, and at the end of the day she’s managed not to have a single breakdown over not grasping the choreography right away. She’s actually in a remarkably good mood, and she accepts Kara’s offer of a home-cooked meal again after rehearsal.

    “Carbonara again?” Kara says with a wink, shutting the lights off and following Lena to the stairwell in their familiar pattern. “I can try to make you cabbage rolls but I remember them being a little labour-intensive.”

    “Or you just could let me cook,” Lena suggests. Kara looks like she’s never considered that possibility before.

    “Now that is a good idea. What would you make?”

    “Whatever you want.”

    “Nachos,” Kara says without hesitation.

    Lena laughs. Kara holds the door open for her, and like clockwork Kara loads her bike into the trunk of Lena’s towncar. “I offer to make you whatever you want, and you ask for nachos?”

    “I said the first thing that came to mind!” Kara says, snapping the trunk shut decisively. “Clearly my mind is on nachos.”

    They make a stop at the grocery store to grab the necessary ingredients (Kara sneaks ice cream and sprinkles into the cart when Lena isn’t looking) and head to Kara’s place, and after Kara points out where all her kitchen necessities are Lena makes herself comfortable chopping jalapenos at Kara’s counter. They’ve been breaking their initial rule of only hanging out at the hotel more and more recently, but when it means she gets to soak up being in Kara’s space for the precious time she has left here Lena can’t find it in herself to care.

    “I didn’t realize you liked to cook. But I guess I never asked,” Kara notes. She’s browning the ground beef at Lena’s instruction, and following her directions to the letter.

    “I don’t get much opportunity to cook with my schedule and living alone,” Lena says. The queso is warming at the back of the stove and Lena gives the knife a quick wash, moving on to chopping tomatoes and onions. “But I enjoy it. I do a lot of meals for one.”

    “A meal with me is like a meal for six,” Kara says with a grin. She shows Lena the pan of meat, and when Lena gives an approving nod she dumps in Lena’s carefully pre-mixed bowl of seasonings. She stirs it for a second, but it doesn’t take long for her to frown. “Lenaaaa. The spices are all sticking to the bottom.”

    “Put a bit of water in,” Lena says when Kara makes a noise of discontent over the soon-to-be-burnt spices, putting her knife down and nudging Kara out of the way with a hip to show her. “Use it to scrape the bits off the bottom and mix them in.”

    “Won’t that make it watery?”

    The pan sizzles when the water hits it, and Lena carefully scrapes the toasted spices up and incorporates them with the meat while Kara watches.

    “It evaporates and gets glossy. It’s called deglazing.”

    Kara takes the wooden spatula back, taking over Lena’s mixing so she can go back to starting on the guacamole. Kara is uncharacteristically hesitant in the kitchen when she’s making something unfamiliar, and it’s so deeply opposed to her confidence in the dance studio. When she’s teaching Lena to dance she knows exactly how to move, how to explain it in a way that’s understandable – here, when she’s doing something as simple as cooking ground beef, she has to ask Lena for assurance that she’s doing it right three separate times. It’s a total inversion of what their relationship has been so far.

    It's strangely thrilling to be the confident one, for once.

    “Are you secretly a chef?” Kara asks, frowning down at the pan like she doesn’t trust it to do what it’s supposed to. “What other skills are you hiding?”

    “I like figuring out the most efficient way to do things,” Lena shrugs. “Cooking is just chemistry when you think about it. There are rules. You can expand on those rules, try to discover new things, but if it doesn’t work you can fall back to the basics and they’ll always work. It’s comforting.” She puts a finishing garnish of cilantro on the guacamole and covers it with cling film, setting it in Kara’s fridge next to heaping bowls of salsa and sour cream.

    “Sounds like dance.”

    “Everything comes back to dance with you,” Lena chuckles. She points at the block of cheese on the table for Kara to start grating, and Kara starts on Lena’s least favourite prep work with a smile.

    “I guess. I tend to just figure things out as I go most of the time.”

    “Which is why the only thing you can make is carbonara.”

    “Hey!”

    The nachos go into the oven in a massive food mountain once they’ve been assembled, and Kara bounces on the balls of her feet while she watches the shredded cheese melt through the oven door. She shoves several chips loaded with toppings straight out of the oven into her mouth before Lena has even set the tray down, and her satisfied moan is borderline pornographic.

    There’s no earthly reason Lena’s body should react to that the way it does, given the fact that it’s almost drowned out by crunching. But she’s long since given up on trying to understand Kara’s effect on her.

    “How are you this excited about nachos?” Lena says rather than examine her reaction more closely, taking a much more moderate scoop. Kara scoffs.

    “How are you not? You just made bar food in my kitchen!” Kara says through a full mouth. She somehow manages to fit in another mouthful, perfectly accessorized with even distributions of red, white, and green dips. “If you can make mozzarella sticks I’ll never have to leave the house for good food again!”

    “That would only be true if I lived here,” Lena notes after both chewing and swallowing. Kara does not take her lead.

    “Keep cooking and I might ask you to move in.”

    Kara is obviously joking, but the comment allows for a split second of contemplation of what that might be like before Lena can stop the thought from forming. Living here. Living with Kara in a cozy space, any cozy space, coming home to her every night at a reasonable time and making dinner together instead of working until midnight on takeout and coffee and then dragging herself to her well-furnished but empty apartment. It makes her ache with a longing she can’t quite suppress.

    Kara takes down the entire mountain of nachos all the way down to the last chip. Lena has no idea where she puts it all, but she’s so complimentary of Lena’s fairly basic rendition of bar food that Lena spends all three of their after-dinner games of Battleship contemplating how to make the perfect mozzarella stick.

     

     

    The choreography for the week is more of a challenge for Lena than any of their previous routines, but once they’re rehearsing without Imra and it’s just the two of them alone in the room again she finds it easier to manage. She’s still not used to the nonsensical movements or how they follow the lyrics rather than the notes of the song, but she’s at least able to memorize how and when Kara is going to move around her. It’s strange and new but with Kara’s help she starts to understand what the routine is actually going for. She feels it. Like even though she can’t speak up herself, day by day her body is trying to tell Kara how accurately the routine maps to her own feelings.

    There’s just one thing she can’t nail down.

    “I promise I am going to be here to catch you.”

    Kara’s reassurance doesn’t loosen Lena’s grip on the scaffolding. She clings to it even though she’s in a fairly secure position, peering down at Kara nervously - it’s hardly more than 6 feet off the ground but it still feels shaky, and the ground looks terribly far away.

    “I know you are,” Lena says, squeezing her eyes shut. “For some reason that still doesn’t make me want to jump.”

    It’s a pretty simple thing that Kara is asking her to do. She’s seen similar things done before on stage, large setpieces used as part of the choreography for people to climb or jump on. Contemporary is known for prop work like this. Kara’s vision is for Lena to climb up on this particular setpiece and after some rolling around and limb-flailing up there, throw herself into Kara’s arms near the end of the song. A total loss of control. A freefall. And try as she might, Lena can’t make herself let go of the stupid railing.

    She knows intellectually that Kara will catch her. She trusts Kara not to have created choreography that might get either of them hurt, and even after her disaster with Mike she knows Kara is different. But there’s a reflex she can’t push past that’s telling her not to jump off a 6-foot object, and nothing Kara says is making it go away.  She can do everything up to the jump, can climb up and hang over the side and follow the choreography right up to the point where she’s supposed to leap, but she keeps freezing right at the last moment.

    “If you feel unsafe, we just won’t do it.”

    Even Kara’s usual reliable calm doesn’t ease Lena’s anxiety. She clings even harder, her heart pounding.

    “I can do it,” she insists, despite making no move to prove it. “Just give me a minute.”

    She’s had almost 20 minutes already with no jump in sight, but Kara makes no mention of it. She just reaches up to take one of Lena’s hands, prying it off the wood beam so she can give it a reassuring squeeze. “But you shouldn’t have to. There are tons of other lifts in the routine that you can do just fine. I want you to always feel comfortable telling me your limits, Lena. We’ll cut the jump.”

    As guilty as it makes her feel, Kara’s suggestion is the only thing that finally allows Lena to breathe properly. Her heart slows its frantic pounding and she nods, making to climb slowly off the platform, but when Kara offers her help she takes it gratefully. “I’m sorry. I don’t know why I’m having so much trouble with this.”

    “You have nothing to apologize for.”

    “Even when I’m ruining your choreography you’re more understanding than most people in my life. Why are you so good to me?”

    Kara lifts her from the setpiece like she weighs nothing and sets her down at the base of it, leaving them standing so close that Lena can smell her shampoo. Kara is looking down at her with a strange expression, her brow lightly furrowed.

    “Because you deserve it,” Kara says softly.

    The few inches of air between them suddenly feel like no space at all. Kara is so close, just a half-step away from being pressed to Lena entirely while she gives her this achingly genuine affirmation, and once again Lena can’t breathe. Kara’s words have sucked all the oxygen out of the room. Kara’s mouth opens and closes, works around unspoken words like she’s trying to voice a thought that would rather not be spoken, and Lena somehow wants to hear it just as much as she hopes Kara never finds the words she’s looking for -

    The air returns to the studio when the smartwatch on Kara’s wrist beeps loudly and vibrates. When Kara glances at it the watch flashes a bright red heart and a ‘146 bpm’ across the screen, and Kara puts her wrist back down quickly.

    “Okay! Let’s try it from the top,” Kara says loudly, clearing her throat and hurrying over to her phone to start the song again. “No jump this time.” The back of her neck is crimson, and Lena doesn’t doubt that her own face is the same shade.

    Lena’s struggle with the routine eases a little without the acrobatics. Kara creates a few off-the-cuff moves for her around the base of the setpiece to fill the space where the jump would have been, and though Lena is still embarrassingly stiff in her movements they manage a few run-throughs from start to finish without any complications. Kara is thrilled by the end of the day, and as always she doesn’t hesitate to say so.

    “I know you thought you were going to struggle this week, but you’re totally getting it already,” Kara says happily, shutting off the lights and slinging her bag over her shoulder. “You should be proud of yourself.”

    Lena smiles. She swallows past the warm, contented feeling Kara’s admiration always brings, and follows Kara into the stairwell. “I wouldn’t want to disappoint you.”

    “You could never!”

    “I couldn’t get the jump, though,” Lena points out.

    “Screw the jump. I like it better without it.”

    Lena knows even without looking into her eyes that Kara is lying, but she appreciates the effort even so.

    Lena’s good mood fades a little once Kara has dug her bike out of the janitor’s closet and started wheeling it towards the door. It’s a reminder that they’re not spending the evening together tonight, since Kara is having a long-overdue sister night with Alex. She should be perfectly okay with this – before she met Kara she’d always preferred solitude, and spending every single night with another person would have exhausted her. Now, she’s spent so much time at Kara’s warm, comfortable apartment lately that the idea of going back to her hotel room alone so early feels cold and empty.

    She’s still thinking of ways to prolong the goodbye when she spots Andrea.

    She’s leaning against the outside of the building with a cup in each hand, as posed as ever until Kara spots her too and frowns in her direction. Andrea blanches a little, but she still holds one of the cups out to Lena, looking repentant and uncharacteristically nervous.

    “Earl grey with honey and lemon?”

    “Just honey,” Lena corrects quietly. Andrea winces.

    “Shit.”

    Lena takes the tea anyways. It’s been a long time since Andrea knew her preferences, so remembering Lena’s favoured type of tea at all is more than Lena expected. “Lemon is fine. Thank you.”

    The pause that follows is painfully awkward. Kara’s hand rests on Lena’s lower back but she’s a silent presence, for which Lena is grateful – she’s not even glaring at Andrea. Her frown eased into a careful study of Lena’s face, probably waiting for a cue to tell her what Lena needs. Lena wishes she knew what to tell her.

    “Give me 10 minutes,” Andrea says, breaking the silence. “We don’t even have to sit down anywhere. Just a walk around the block. I really want to clear the air between us before the show is over.”

    Lena bites the inside of her cheek. Kara is still waiting for her lead, and Lena takes sip of the tea to give herself a moment to think – it’s far too sweet and the lemon isn’t her preference, but it’s passable. Kara’s honey ratio is better.

    That thought is strangely soothing.

    “Sure,” Lena finally says, giving Andrea a tight nod. “I have some time.” She gestures to her driver that she’ll be a few minutes and turns to Kara, who has barely looked at Andrea since they came outside. “I’ll talk to you later?”

    Kara nods, a familiar crinkle forming between her brows just before she pulls Lena into a hug. “Call me if you need anything.”

    “I’ll be fine.” Lena squeezes Kara back, pressing a quick kiss to her cheek for good measure while Andrea is watching. “Have a good night with Alex.”

    Kara hops on her bike and heads off, though Lena watches her twist around in her seat to watch them walking in the same direction three separate times before she gets to the end of the street, where she almost runs headlong into a stop sign. Andrea doesn’t speak until Kara has turned the corner.

    “I’m sorry, Lena.”

    Lena had been planning on keeping her eyes on her feet for the duration of this walk, but at that she looks up. She’s known Andrea for a long time, and even back when Andrea had initially betrayed her she hadn’t really apologized so much as tried to explain her actions. She’d always been more the type to do grand gestures rather than apologize with words. When they’d been together Lena had received so many floral arrangements and bottles of conciliatory liquor she could have started her own shop. Hearing her genuinely apologize once the first time they ran into each other here weeks ago was shocking enough. But twice?

    “What?” Lena says, for lack of anything else to say. Andrea’s lips purse.

    “Do I really have to repeat myself?”

    “If you did it would be the third apology I’ve ever gotten from you,” Lena says with a bit of steel. Andrea takes the pointed comment admirably.

    “That’s a little harsh. Though not totally unwarranted.”

    Lena takes another sip of tea. It only makes her wish Kara had made it instead. “What are you sorry for, exactly?”

    It’s a tortuous question to ask someone like Andrea, but Lena isn’t going to be satisfied with a vague apology. As much as she’s been avoiding this, now that she’s here only it feels right to get to the quick of it and hash it all out. Andrea takes up the responsibility with a deep breath.

    “For betraying your trust. For ruining what we had for the sake of my career. For not being there for you when everything happened with your family,” Andrea lists off, twisting her cup in her hands. “For doubting your relationship with Kara. And now, for pushing you to do this so I can clear my conscience.”

    The candour is surprising. It sounds like Andrea rehearsed it in her head, but that doesn’t make it feel any less genuine. She’s had a long time to think about it, Lena supposes – clearly she’s been wanting to get it out for a while.

    “Thank you,” Lena says. “For your apology. I admit that I might have gotten a bit too defensive about Kara.”

    Andrea drums her fingers on her cup. “I was wrong about your relationship. I shouldn’t have questioned you. I told myself I was trying to help you out, but I…” she trails off, clenching her fist until little fissures form in the waxed cardboard. “I was jealous.”

    Damn. Kara was right after all. She’s going to be insufferably smug if Lena ever decides to tell her.

    “But I had no actual basis for what I said besides this gut feeling,” Andrea continues, unaware of exactly how right her gut feeling was. “It’s obvious to anyone with eyes that it’s not a showmance. Kara is madly in love with you.”

    Andrea says it casually, with an almost-bitter little laugh as she sips her drink and turns the corner to loop the block. But Lena’s stomach swoops so dramatically that she trips over an uneven crack in the sidewalk, almost dropping her tea all over the street.

    Love?

    “She is?” Lena finds herself saying. She knows as soon as it leaves her mouth that she hasn’t even remotely disguised the yearning in her voice, and Andrea picks it up at once; she stops dead, and when Lena looks up Andrea is staring at her. Hard.

    “Lena?”

    Lena says nothing. Her heart is pounding - she could probably give Kara’s 146 bpm a run for its money.

    “Oh my god,” Andrea says softly, so softly. So reminiscent of the girl she was when Lena still trusted her. “Lena.”

    Lena can’t be sure why she doesn’t immediately protest like she did last time. Andrea might not believe her, but after this conversation she would probably take Lena at her word. It’s the safe option, the only sane option. But maybe Lena needs to hear an opinion from someone outside her bubble. Someone who isn’t Sam or Jess or Alex. Andrea might just be the worst person on earth she could trust, and yet Lena wants to. She wants to talk to the person she used to confide everything in. Andrea has always been intuitive and devastatingly blunt - maybe she can finally give Lena a perspective she’s been missing.

    Or maybe Lena is just finally getting desperate and self-destructive enough to give Andrea the ammunition she needs to topple this whole thing. 

    Andrea doesn’t have the hungry look in her eyes that Lena remembers from the last time she spilled a secret. She just looks concerned, and faced with that achingly strange familiarity Lena can’t deny it anymore. She grabs Andrea by the sleeve and pulls her off the sidewalk to the closest alley, shoving her far enough back that nobody from the street could eavesdrop.

    “If you really want me to trust you again, consider this your first test.”

    “Are you serious?” Andrea whisper-yells, and Lena levels a pointed finger at her chest.

    “Tell anyone about this, even infer that you know anything, and my legal team will bury you in litigation so deep – “

    “I won’t!” Andrea says. Lena points harder, and Andrea puts her hands up in surrender. “Lena, I swear. I won’t make that mistake ever again. Sorry, I just - I can’t believe I was right?”

    It feels so much like it used to before all their complicated romantic entanglements destroyed their relationship, when they were just best friends who shared all their secrets. Andrea’s promise heals a part of her she didn’t expect.

    Lena takes a big breath. “It started out as a temporary thing, but I’ve been here so much longer than I expected and it’s just snowballed and –”

    “And you’re in love with her.”

    All the breath she worked so hard for leaves her at once.

    “No,” Lena says, her voice thin and unconvincing. “I’m telling you that it’s fake.”

    Andrea seems to weigh her next words carefully. When she speaks, it’s slow and measured. “It’s been a long time, but I think I still know your tells. Whether you want me to or not. Your relationship may be fake, but…Lena, you’re in love with her.”

    There’s an undercurrent of hurt to her words, but more predominant is empathy. And it’s too much for Lena to handle. She turns away from Andrea’s understanding eyes, facing the opposite brick wall and pressing her fingers into the bridge of her nose.

    “I am so screwed.”

    “Why?”

    “Why?” Lena says, whirling back around. “Because I have feelings for someone who lives halfway across the country, and who I’ve been steadily assuring for almost two months that I have no intentions beyond friendship. Someone who has given me no indication that she wants anything serious. And apparently I’m broadcasting it to the entire fucking country.”

    Andrea looks thoughtful. After a moment she steps back out onto the sidewalk, tilting her head for Lena to follow, which she does on shaky legs.

    “She clearly feels the same way, so what’s the issue?” Andrea asks in a lower voice. “You have to have seen your own performances. She looks at you like -”

    “I know she harbours some kind of attraction. Sam has been telling me since the first week,” Lena says curtly. “The issue is that anything beyond that is totally unrealistic.”

    “Like she worships the ground you walk on,” Andrea finishes, overriding Lena’s interruption. “I wasn’t kidding when I said she was madly in love with you. And she doesn’t just do it onstage.” She takes a sip of tea, her lips pursing. “It drove me insane to watch.”

    There’s a lump in Lena’s throat she can’t seem to swallow past. She’s known in the back of her mind since Monday night that there could be more than attraction to the way Kara treats her, but it’s so much easier to keep to the simple explanation. She should have expected that Andrea would blow that wide open.

    “Basic chemistry is different than…” Lena can’t bring herself to say the word Andrea used. The one that immediately springs to mind when she thinks too hard about Kara. “Than emotional attachment. And it’s not enough to sustain a relationship.”

    “I’d almost forgotten how clinical you can be when you’re scared.” Andrea’s voice is soft, not confrontational, and as they round another corner Lena finds herself slowing her pace to keep the walk going longer. “Believe me or don’t, Lee. I don’t care. But you’re deluding yourself if you think she wouldn’t jump at the snap of your fingers.”

    “I really do hate that nickname these days, you know,” Lena says halfheartedly. “You’re the only one who ever called me that.” But there’s no venom behind it, and Andrea lets out a breath that almost feels like a laugh.

    “Noted.”

    “I don’t know what to do,” Lena admits. She says it to the sky, looking up at the dusky rising moon and wondering if Kara is looking out her window at it too. It’s a terribly romantic and deeply stupid thing to think, and yet it makes her heart ache a little. “Sam wants me to go for it, Alex tells me not to, and I don’t even know which option I actually want. I’m…terrified.”

    “That’s my fault, isn’t it?”

    Lena laughs humorlessly. “Not exclusively. I imagine that some blame can be laid with my family.”

    Andrea half-smiles. All that can be heard for a few steps is the slow clicking of Andrea’s heeled boots before she finally offers her opinion.

    “I know what happened between us…” Andrea pauses, and then continues in a softer and more contrite voice, “what I did to you. It’s probably made it hard to trust. I can’t imagine how difficult it would be to open yourself up to someone after I broke your heart like that.”

    “Someone’s giving herself a little too much credit.”

    Andrea does laugh this time, low and quiet. They both know it’s not an exaggeration – Lena’s heart had been broken, and it hadn’t seemed to finish mending until now. Until she met Kara.

    “I don’t want my mistake to keep you from finding happiness,” Andrea says. Her next words seem difficult to spit out. “It’s hard to trust again, but. Kara seems like a good person to take that risk on.”

    They’ve come right back around to the front doors of the studio by the time Andrea is finished, their loop around the block complete. Lena’s driver is still waiting but she doesn’t open the car door right away. She lingers, toying with the string of her teabag.

    “I’m not good at risks.”

    “You take risks with your company all the time. I’ve been watching your career, you know,” Andrea says, her voice sliding into warmth. “Taking over L-Corp was a massive risk, and you did it fearlessly.”

    “That’s different. I knew all the variables,” Lena argues. “I have power there. I know how everything works. I don’t take risks in my personal life. I’ve had the same two friends my whole adulthood, and they’re both my employees.”

    “Doing this show was a risk,” Andrea counters.

    “A risk I was pushed into by the aforementioned friend-employees.”

    Andrea doesn’t let up. She’s just as tenacious as ever, and she doesn’t look keen to go anywhere until she’s finished making Lena squirm under interrogation. Just like old times. “You’ve made friends here, too.”

    “Because of Kara.”

    “Lena,” Andrea says, exasperation bleeding through. “You can’t hide from the world forever.”

    “I disagree. It’s worked until now.”

    “Exactly. Until now.”

    Lena sighs. She doesn’t have a counterpoint to that, and Andrea knows it.

    “I won’t lie and say I’d be disappointed if you didn’t go for it,” Andrea says, fixing her gaze determinedly somewhere over Lena’s left shoulder for the admission. “I don’t think it’s ever going to not hurt to see you with someone else, even knowing it’ll never work between us again. But it would hurt more to see you continue to be miserable because of what I did to you.”

    It’s a fair point. If there’s one thing Lena has realized since she’s been in National City, it’s that her life in Metropolis was unfulfilling. She’s spent most of her adulthood overworked and lonely, convincing herself she didn’t need anything besides her drive to rehabilitate her family’s company, and ever since she started spending time with Kara and enjoying things outside her job she’s been happier than she can ever remember being. Even with the emotional turmoil.

    Maybe she does want to be with Kara. But every time she so much as thinks about making that decision she can’t make herself do it. Their dance this week is infuriatingly apt – she can’t make the stupid jump. She isn’t brave enough.

    “I’ll think about it,” Lena concedes.

    “Good.”

    Lena isn’t sure what to do next. She’d love nothing more than to go home and lie in the dark, to think about the strange turn her night took, but it seems rude to be the one to end the conversation - thankfully Andrea takes the lead.

    “I’m glad we did this. Trying to be friends again might be too much too soon, but maybe next time I’m in Metropolis we can get lunch. Start slow.”

    Lena nods, relieved. “I’d like that.”

    Andrea hails the next cab, and Lena gets into her own car in a haze. She spends most of the drive and a good portion of the rest of her night thinking in endless circles, never landing on a solid conclusion.

One of the less reputable gossip sites publishes a photo of Lena walking closely with Andrea under a splashy headline that makes her roll her eyes, speculating that Lena’s romantic attention has moved from Kara to a new target. Nobody of any consequence picks it up but it handily ruins Lena’s morning nonetheless, and she has to assume that Kara saw it too considering the first thing that comes out of her mouth when Lena arrives for rehearsal.

She’s fidgety after she’s unlocked their studio door, snapping her fingers incessantly as she watches Lena put her things down on the windowsill, and Lena stops to lean against the frame and pin her with a pointed look.

    “Nothing,” Kara says, her hands not stilling. “Why would anything be wrong?”

    “No, Kara,” Lena says more loudly, following Kara’s progress across the floor with her eyes with some incredulity. She’s not used to seeing Kara so agitated. “Did you forget the part where she gave me permanent trust issues? Do you really think I’d be able to go down that road again?”

As much as Lena wants to be able to say it’s categorically untrue, Kara is not hiding her jealousy very well. And it’s making Lena’s face feel warm.

    Lena puts jealousy and warmth out of her mind. This is about strategy, and Lena can handle that. How she feels about Kara’s hint of possessiveness over their (fake) relationship is irrelevant.

    “That’s probably a good idea,” Lena says, following along with the stretches. “Too bad, though. I was enjoying eating home-cooked meals.”

    “What is?”

    “We can cook at my place!”

If it means she gets another night in Kara’s orbit she can muddle through cooking on camera. Kara is a cheerful bullet train. She’s rolling along at top speed, already making plans while Lena tries to keep up - after rehearsal is over they hit the grocery store again, and a mere hour later Lena is preparing to make gnocchi while Kara sets her phone up on a tripod on the kitchen island.

They’re moving so quickly, only to be replaced by more, that it’s impossible to keep track. It doesn’t do much to make her feel less nervous, but Kara’s warm, proud smile does. Most of them are professions of love and excitement to see them together or compliments and Lena dusts the flour from her hands.

She doesn’t want their time to end. She wants to have nights like this with Kara all the time, every night, for the foreseeable future.

    “It’s not if,” Kara says, jabbing her spoon in Lena’s direction. “It’s when.”

    “I think it all depends on if I manage to loosen up before Monday night,” Lena mutters. While she’s fully mastered the beats of the choreography she’s still been struggling all week with the stylistic parts that require loose, emotional movement. The moments of soft intimacy that come so easily to Kara. The parts that sell the love story. The only way to keep herself sane while acting out such a romantic ideal with Kara is to shut herself off from it, which throttles the impact of the choreography completely.

    She’s come a long way, but letting go of the rigidity that’s kept her safe her entire life is difficult to do in just a week - at this point, the solution is either a slightly stiff performance or some sort of miraculous breakthrough on stage.

    “I know you have it in you,” Kara says easily. “Maybe it’s a muscle thing - have you tried getting a massage?”

    Lena shakes her head, chuckling. “That’s a solid no.”

    “I could just give you one. They’re really helpful.” Kara hardly seems to be paying attention, focused on scooping up the last of the gnocchi, but Lena’s stomach does a somersault.

    “So you’re a dancer and a massage therapist?” Lena says. She starts to gather the dishes just to have something to do with her hands, but Kara’s sink is tiny and once they’re all stacked on the counter next to it there isn’t much left to do besides start washing them, which feels rude mid-conversation.

    “Most of us know at least a little for self-treatment,” Kara shrugs. “And to help each other out backstage. I’m happy to do it.”

    Lena’s first instinct is another unequivocal no. But the little voice from before that reminded her of her lack of remaining time with Kara returns with a vengeance while Kara finishes off the food, whispering things she wouldn’t normally consider. Like how after next week, she might not see Kara again. This could be the last real chance to be close to her. Kara has already given her small massages, rubbed her shoulders or her calves without issue when she cramps up during rehearsal. This shouldn’t be much different. And she is very tense.

    Maybe this will help.

    “Okay,” Lena says, throwing herself on the mercy of the whim.

    Kara has her laid out on her stomach on the bed with her relaxation playlist filtering in from the living room in a few whirlwind minutes, and Lena can’t stop herself from seizing up as the mattress dips beside her where Kara is kneeling to put her hands gently on Lena’s mid-back over her shirt.

    “Jeez, Lena. You’re more tense than I thought,” Kara says, her fingers light while she searches out the worst spots to focus on.     “Except you.”

    “Oh,” Kara repeats, but her tone is much different this time. Lower. More breathy. Her hands return, and Lena tries her best to relax into them. “Okay. If you’re sure.”

    “I am. Though I’m not sure if this is helping,” Lena says, squirming a little. “My shirt feels odd.”

Sometimes fabric can be a little overstimulating.” Kara doesn’t finish that thought, doesn’t so much as imply anything else, but Lena’s brain finishes it anyways – it’s easier to do it without. Lena knows this, and it’s one of the major reasons she’s never gotten this done by a professional. Keeping her shirt on is a key part of all this not being too much to bear. The rational part of her brain knows just how slippery a slope she’s on, hovering on the edge of having Kara’s hands on her bare skin, but that deeper, more base part of her mind is whispering through it. If they manage to get through this week and into the top 3, next week will be her last in National City. Her last opportunity to experience anything to do with Kara. Physical touch, or otherwise. Shouldn’t she make the most of it while she’s here?

    “I could take it off,” Lena finds herself saying. She hears her own voice as if she’s an outsider, an echo that feels divorced from her actual mouth even as it says what she’s thinking. Kara freezes again, and Lena’s heart jumps into her throat.

    “If you’re comfortable,” Lena amends, backpedaling as fast as she can. “I understand if you aren’t, it’s not -”

    “I am!” Kara says loudly. It cuts Lena off at the pass, and she clears her throat in the sudden quiet it leaves. “Uh, I am. Totally comfortable. Anything to help you feel better, right? I have some lotion?”

    Lena’s heart, now settled back in her chest, starts to race.

    “Sure. Whatever you think will work.”

    Lena removes her shirt while Kara is grabbing the lotion from the bathroom, and in just her bra she situates herself to lie face-down down with her arms folded above her head for when Kara returns. There’s clattering coming from the bathroom that seems a bit prolonged for just grabbing a bottle of lotion, and the song changes from one Lena doesn’t recognize to one she does – Fleetwood Mac. One of her favourites, soft and infused with longing. She wonders, in the back of her racing mind, if Kara made this playlist herself.

    She can hear Kara’s footsteps approaching, but just as the song rises into the second verse they stop at the threshold of the bedroom. The gentle piano of the song continues, Christine McVie crooning directly to Lena’s heart, and the stillness goes on just long enough that Lena almost twists around to make sure Kara is still in the room – it’s only when she shifts that she finally hears Kara approaching again, and the reality of the situation hits her fully.

    She’s shirtless in Kara’s bed, and Kara is about to have her hands everywhere.

    Kara seems more careful when she climbs onto the bed this time, more hesitant, and Lena squeezes her eyes shut and presses her face into the mattress when Kara’s warm hands spread over her bare skin. They’re firm and a little slick with lotion – vanilla and coconut, Lena notes absently – and she applies pressure in a few different places until she hits a spot that makes Lena grunt and seize up.

    “Fuck!” Lena gasps. It’s a straight shot of pain from the spot Kara is pressing on right over to her spine, a lightning bolt that takes her breath away. She considers herself to have a pretty high pain tolerance, but the suddenness of it is more than she expected.

    “Oh, wow. There it is,” Kara murmurs, easing up and running her thumb more gently around the area to feel it out. “Holy crap, Lena, this knot feels like it’s been here your whole life.”

    “It probably has,” Lena says tightly. The acute pain has faded to something duller, and Kara hums.

    “Is it okay if I work on it? It might hurt a little, but it’ll feel better after.”

    “Work away,” Lena says, clenching her hands into fists in anticipation of pain.

    “Okay. Can you maybe relax for me?” Kara says, and Lena huffs a laugh, unclenching her hands and trying to do the relaxation breathing exercises Kara taught her in her first week.

    “I’ll try.”

    Kara works diligently for long minutes as the song fades out and bleeds into another, but Lena is past paying attention to the music. Kara’s hands are magic. She has no concept of exactly what techniques she’s using, if any, but as much as her body is resisting it Lena can feel it working already. Kara’s talented fingers are dancing around the worst spot, dancing and dancing until finally they do something that makes the knot loosen a bit, and the sheer physical release it brings is so deep-seated in her that Lena actually moans into the sheets. It’s long and low and completely unintended, and it feels like it comes from somewhere that’s only just now been revealed by Kara’s hands.

    “That’s good?” Kara says. Her voice is raspy, her hands still working, and Lena tries to breathe through the sensation of what feels like half the muscles of her back loosening for the first time since she was a child. It makes her eyes water with a strange surge of emotion, like what Kara is untangling is the basis of every stressor in her life. Like everything she’s carried for years is stored there and Kara is loosening it bit by bit.

    “Yeah,” Lena says in what can only be accurately described as a whimper.

    Kara takes that as a green light - she keeps going, digging deeper into knots that feel like they’re lodged in Lena’s very being, and Lena is shocked to find that the longer it goes, the more her brain is processing the pain as something else. It feels good. It feels borderline spiritual. She can’t keep herself quiet, can’t tamp down the probably deeply inappropriate noises she’s making, and she can’t find it in herself to care. She hardly even hears herself at all. She’s floating free somewhere, being suspended by the surety of Kara’s tireless hands.

    Lena is practically catatonic when Kara moves away. She recognizes the absence, though, and Kara’s soft voice floats in through the haze.

    “I’m gonna stop, okay? If I keep going you might be too sore to dance tomorrow.”

    Lena just groans into the mattress. She hears Kara laugh a little to herself, and she’s half-aware that she’s now being draped under a blanket – she’s so lethargic that she doesn’t spare a thought to pyjamas, or putting a shirt on, or the fact that it’s Kara’s bed she’s being carefully tucked into. She’s drifting off before the covers are settled over her.

    She’s just barely aware, in a dreamlike way, of Kara pressing a lingering kiss to her temple and whispering something she can’t quite hear before she’s dead to the world.

    It takes an embarrassingly long time for Lena to register where she is when she wakes up. There’s bright sunlight behind her eyelids and quiet clattering coming from somewhere to her right, and the pillow her face is pressed into smells familiar. Like her own perfume, which she doesn’t remember wearing yesterday, but also…Kara’s shampoo?

    Lena cracks one eye open.

    She’s in Kara’s bed, which explains the source of the shampoo scent. Kara’s covers are folded both under and over her, swaddling her like a downy taco without moving her from where she was lying last night, and she can see a messy pile of blankets on the couch. The noise is identified when Kara strides into view in sweats and a tank top, sipping what smells like fresh coffee from a mug with Tigger printed on the side. Her hair still wet from a morning shower. She hasn’t noticed yet that Lena is awake, and Lena takes a moment to just observe her in her natural habitat. She’s humming to herself, straightening the blankets on the couch and cleaning up their dishes from last night, and even as she moves casually around her own space it almost looks like she’s dancing.

    It makes Lena’s chest expand with an affection she doesn’t bother to contain. A perfect morning, really. Something she wishes she could savour for longer than a few minutes.

    Lena sits up slowly, taking stock of her body – she’s a little sore, but in a way that actually makes her feel more limber. The lack of tension in her back is new, certainly. She’s also still in her bra and leggings, and she tugs the blanket up to cover herself even though Kara hasn’t noticed her yet.

    “Did you sleep on the couch?” Lena asks in lieu of a greeting. Kara jumps, almost spilling her coffee, but her smile brightens when she sees Lena awake.

    “Hey, you’re up!” Kara says, her voice as chipper as always. “Good morning! I’m making smoothies, but I didn’t want to wake you.” Kara disappears past the wall that separates the bed from the kitchen and Lena follows her, grabbing her shirt from the floor and slipping it on. She’s just turned the corner when Kara turns the blender on, whizzing a bunch of ingredients together and pouring the finished product into two travel cups and notably not answering Lena’s question. “We’ve gotta head out soon if we want to stop at the hotel so you can change.”

    “I didn’t mean to kick you out of your bed,” Lena says, rubbing the sleep from her eyes. She feels oddly okay with Kara seeing her first thing in the morning now, makeup-less and with messy hair. Kara sees her sweaty and struggling in rehearsal and doesn’t seem to think any less of her.

    “Oh, you didn’t! Sometimes the couch is better for my back,” Kara says, not entirely convincingly. She offers a travel cup to Lena, poking a metal straw through the opening. “Strawberry banana?”

    Lena chuckles, taking the cup. “I don’t usually eat breakfast.”

    “You dance all the way to lunch with no breakfast?”

    While she texts her driver the change of morning plans and Kara grabs her bag in preparation to leave, Lena tastes the smoothie. It’s actually pretty nice – not too sweet, mostly yogurt and fruit, with some chia seeds on top. “Before I met you I sometimes went a whole day without eating if work was busy enough.”

    Kara tuts. “That’s a terrible habit! Your body needs nutrients!”

    Kara continues to evangelize the benefits of a regular eating schedule when she locks the door behind them, spending most of their shared wait and subsequent drive discussing the link between nutrient intake and energy efficiency, and she thankfully doesn’t mention anything that happened last night. Not Lena’s humiliatingly emotional reaction to something as simple as a massage, or the fact that Lena slept like a baby in her bed and woke up more well-rested and pain-free than she’s felt in years.

    And Lena, in turn, doesn’t mention that Kara’s pillows smell like Dior.

    The whole morning feels comfortable and almost domestic in a way that Lena didn’t expect from her accidental sleepover. Kara sprawls out on the hotel bed and scrolls on her phone while Lena showers and changes in the bathroom, and even though they start rehearsal a solid hour and a half later than usual it’s still the most productive day they’ve had in the studio yet this week. Lena even admits, when they’re packing up at the end of the day, that the massage and breakfast might have helped.

    Kara brings an extra smoothie along with the usual tea to rehearsal in the morning every day for the rest of the week.

     

     

    The hours preceding the broadcast this week are some of the calmest and quietest that Lena has ever experienced here. With most of the couples gone the energy is less frantic than usual, and with no more suspicious stares from Andrea and even Siobhan’s usual sass quieted by what seems like an uncharacteristic bout of nerves the hair and makeup trailer is almost silent. Lena spends it in a meditative state, going over the choreography in her head and letting the artists work their magic.

    Maybe it’s the long strangeness of the week or maybe it’s just two months worth of experience under her belt, but even though she’s not nearly as confident with this routine as she has been with their past ones she’s also not nearly as nervous as she thought she might be. While Siobhan paces in circles in the green room and even Andrea anxiously nitpicks at James’ costume while they wait for the countdown to live broadcast Lena is tranquil, sitting with a cup of tea from craft services on one of the leather couches with Kara while the blonde shows her some of the endless messages of support they’ve been getting online.

    It feels almost perfectly opposed to how she felt during swap week with Mike. She’d been tranquil in a way, then, because she was so sure that she was going to be eliminated that it felt like a premonition. An inevitability that she couldn’t stop. She feels that same inevitability now, but in the opposite direction – it feels like something is different, an energy change she can’t explain properly, but somehow despite any evidence to the contrary it feels like everything is going to be okay.

    Maybe it’s the after-effects of her first massage talking. It seems strange for the calmness to extend so many days past the actual experience, but she’s no expert.

    Their costumes are the simplest they’ve ever been. Lena is surprisingly comfortable in a flowy pastel gossamer dress which swishes around her thighs much like her loose hair does down her back, kept modest by a concealing leotard underneath; Kara looks like a textbook romance novel cover model in loose pants and a white linen shirt, with blonde curls cascading over her shoulders. It’s the first time since the first week that they’ve both had loose hair onstage, and Lena knows Kara must have requested it for the aesthetic impact of how they’ll look whirling together during the countless jumps and spins. They’re set to dance between Kelly and Max’s jazz routine and Andrea and James’ waltz, and when the time comes Lena toes out of her flats at the edge of the stage to stand barefoot with Kara and wait for their cue.

    “Ready?” Kara asks, her fingers laced tightly in Lena’s. Her voice is breathy, exhilarated like the moment before a rollercoaster hits the big drop. Lena wiggles her toes, squeezing back hard.

    “Ready.”

    Alex waves them on, and she takes her place in their starting position – an intimate hug, Lena’s face tucked into Kara’s neck – as the video starts far above them.

    A good portion of it this time seems to be focusing on last week’s triumph rather than what they’re doing this week – it starts with a highlight reel of their paso doble and the comments they received, cutting to the interview questions Lena remembers answering earlier in the week.

    “Last week was a record-breaker. How do you plan on living up to the new bar you’ve set?”

    “I mean, I guess it’s like a ladder, right?” Kara says confidently on-screen, her arm slung over the back of Lena’s chair in the studio. “You just keep trying to go up until there’s no more ladder left. And then you try to stay there as long as you can.”

    “Hopefully it keeps going until you’re in the top 3!” The interviewer says, focusing now on Lena. Lena remembers he had been a little fixated on getting her comments on everything as well as Kara’s, for some reason. “Are you excited about being contenders for the trophy?”
